# Who Do They Answer To? Ten atlas directions from scratch

Date: 30 October 2026  
For: Dustin Cathcart, Founder and Editor, Oath & Action  
Review board: `work/boards/phase-2/atlas-concepts/ideas/20261030_From-Scratch-Directions-Sketches.html` (capture: `20261030_From-Scratch-Directions-Sketches.png` beside it)  
Built by: `work/boards/phase-2/atlas-concepts/ideas/build_sketches.py`  
Sources read: `work/boards/phase-2/maps/20261028_Maps-Model-Data.json` and `work/boards/phase-2/atlas-concepts/shared/20261030_Variable-Icon-Map.json`, plus the Civic Line SVGs.

## What the data says

I studied the model before sketching. These findings shaped the ten structures.

- The five core areas pass influence around a circle. Between neighboring areas in the order A, B, C, D, E and back to A, 20 connections run forward and 11 run back.
- 34 variables form one knot in which each can reach every other. The other 17 only push into it. Housing scarcity and owner home equity form their own small loop (R8) outside the knot.
- All 23 loops sit in areas A to E. The economy layer, the technology group and the outside drivers hold no loop, and 26 variables sit in no loop at all.
- Policy tilt to insiders (E1) sits in 7 loops and has 13 connections, the most of any variable. Electoral pressure (C3) and incumbent control of election rules (C4) sit in four loops each.
- L42, "Incumbent-drawn maps create safe seats", is the only connection in three loops (R4a, R10, R12).
- 46 pairs of loops share at least one variable. R8, B2 and B3 share none.
- Following connections backward, all 51 variables reach households' financial security (A4) within 6 steps, and 8 act on it directly.
- 12 connections cross between the economy (A and the economy layer) and democracy (B to E): 5 toward democracy, 7 toward the economy. Four loops cross: R1, R5, R6 and R15.
- Loops differ in ways a drawing can carry: 12 have two steps, 7 three and 4 four; 18 reinforce and 5 balance; each step has its own delay and evidence grade; and the model gives each loop a state, from running up to switched off.

Every sketch below follows the brand rules: only the seven brand colors, one working red at the point the headline names, and evidence drawn as line texture (solid for documented and well-supported, dashed for plausible, dash-dot for contested, dotted for hypothesis). The three sketched loops, which have no numbered chain to grade, draw as a hollow band so that no texture claims a grade they lack.

## 1. Great Circle

The five core areas drawn as one ring, because influence in the model travels around a circle.

**Big idea.** Take the connections between neighboring core areas in the order A, B, C, D, E and back to A. Of these, 20 run forward around that circle and 11 run back. In the model, money and organized interests bear on elections, and elections decide who governs. What government does then shapes whose interests policy favors, and that tilt feeds back into who holds wealth. A ring shows this circle before the reader learns a single variable name. Each of the 26 core variables gets a fixed place on the rim, each connection between them becomes a chord, and each loop becomes a closed shape inside the ring.

**Overview.** The ring carries areas A to E as five labeled arcs. Inside it run the 61 connections among those 26 variables. The 46 that belong to a loop draw in Prussian, or Verdigris for balancing loops, and the other 15 draw in thin Stone. The five outside drivers sit beyond the rim with short arrows to the variables they push. The economy layer (12 variables) and the technology group (8) appear as two closed discs outside the ring, each with a count and a bundle of lines, and a tap opens either one as a ring of its own. The overview leaves out names, signs and delays until the pointer rests on something. Readers click the arcs. They are the five largest shapes on the screen, they carry the area titles in display type, and they thicken under the pointer.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking C turns the ring until C sits at the top and widens its arc to half the circle. Its five variables get icons and labels. Eight connections arrive from other areas and seven leave, and they enter and exit as chords from the faded rest of the ring. R10 is the four-sided shape inside the arc. Three of its chords are solid, because C1 to C2 and C4 to C1 are documented and C2 to C3 is well supported. The fourth, C3 to C4, is the weakest step, graded plausible, and draws dashed. The R10 page draws the same shape at full size, sets each step's sentence along its chord and puts the delay outside the rim: under two years for the first two steps, 2 to 10 years for the last two.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail is the ring in miniature with one loop drawn on it. The areas the loop touches widen, as under a magnifier, and the ring turns so the loop keeps the direction it has on the full map. Variables keep their order, so the data sets the shape. R8 is a lens inside area A. R10 is a four-sided figure filling the widened C arc, and R12 is a long kite from the courts in D to safe seats in C. R1 is a triangle spanning A, B and E. Balancing loops draw in Verdigris and reinforcing loops in Prussian. The three sketched loops have no numbered chain, so they appear as a hollow band near the center with open points. On the all-loops page the 23 rings sit in a grid, and a reader tells them apart by where the shape sits on the rim and how far across it reaches.

**Ground and red.** Newsprint ground, Charcoal rim, Prussian and Verdigris loop chords, Stone for everything else. The one red marks a chord. On the overview it marks L42, "Incumbent-drawn maps create safe seats", the only connection inside three loops (R4a, R10, R12) and the place where R-03 and R-04 act.

**Risks.** A chord diagram turns into a hairball fast. With 61 chords, the ones outside loops have to stay faint until a reader asks for them. Order on the rim follows the areas, so neighbors on the rim can look related when no connection joins them. The two discs hide 20 variables behind a tap, and readers may take them as minor. On a phone the ring fits at about 340 pixels across. Arcs and loop shapes survive at that size; names move to a list under the ring.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## 2. Star Atlas

Variables as stars and loops as named constellations, on a dark sky divided into regions.

**Big idea.** Printed star atlases taught the public to find order in a crowded sky by drawing figures between stars and naming them. This model poses the same problem, with 51 variables and 118 connections, and 23 patterns that matter most. Each variable becomes a star whose size follows its number of connections, so policy tilt to insiders, with 13, shines brightest. The sky splits into regions, one per area, with straight stepped borders like the ones astronomers draw. Each loop becomes a constellation with its title. A first-time reader can learn one figure and find it again on the next visit.

**Overview.** The whole sky shows on one screen with its stars, regions and 23 figures. Star positions come from a layout that pulls connected variables together and keeps each area in its region. Figure lines draw only for loop steps. The 72 connections that belong to no loop stay hidden until the reader picks a star. Region names run in small capitals inside each region. The reader clicks a figure or a region: figures brighten under the pointer and show their title, and region names act as targets.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking Electoral competition zooms the sky to that region. Its five stars grow and take icons and labels. Connections that leave the region run off as lines toward faint neighboring stars, labeled with where they go. R10 is the four-star figure of safe seats, primary decisiveness, electoral pressure and incumbent control of election rules. The line from electoral pressure to incumbent control is dashed because that step is plausible, and the other three are solid. On the R10 page the figure stands alone at full size with each line captioned by its sentence, and the faint stars around it keep its place in the sky.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail crops the sky to one figure and scales it to fit. Positions are real, so the shapes differ. R13 and R18 are slim lenses inside the governing region, R12 is a long four-star figure from the courts across to safe seats, and R6 bends through three regions. Star sizes carry over, so any loop through policy tilt to insiders has one bright star in it. The three sketched loops have no stars to stand on and appear as hollow rings with open points.

**Ground and red.** Charcoal ground, Newsprint stars, White lines for reinforcing figures, Verdigris for balancing ones, Stone for region borders and for stars outside every loop. The one red is a star. In the sketch it is C2, primary decisiveness, where R-01, all-party primaries, acts.

**Risks.** Star positions come from an algorithm, so distance on the sky means little, and readers trained by real maps will read meaning into it. Hiding the 72 non-loop connections makes the system look simpler than the model says. Some figures share stars, and R4a and R12 overlap on three of them, so the overview must light one figure at a time. A phone can show the whole sky as a picture, but picking a single star needs a zoom step first.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## 3. Ledger Grid

Every connection as a cell in a table of causes and effects; loops become staircases.

**Big idea.** A table answers one question per cell: when the row rises, what happens to the column? The model fits that form, since every connection runs from one variable to another with a sign. Rows hold causes and columns hold effects. Each area forms a block on the diagonal. The overview folds the 51 by 51 table into a 10 by 10 table of areas, and the circle through the core areas shows up as the cells just above the diagonal plus one in the far corner. A loop in a table has an exact drawing. Go across a row to the cell of the connection, drop to the diagonal, and go across again. Each loop becomes a closed staircase.

**Overview.** The overview shows ten rows and ten columns, one per area, in the order A, B, C, D, E, then the outside drivers, the three economy groups and technology. Each cell holds one dot per connection, Prussian for connections in a loop and Stone for the rest. Cells A to B, B to C, C to D, D to E and E to A carry an outline so the forward circle is visible: 5, 4, 4, 4 and 3 connections. The table leaves out signs, delays and names at this level. Every cell with dots is a target, and the pointer turns it into a sentence such as "C to D: 4 connections, 3 in loops".

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking the C block opens a five by five table of its variables, with a strip on each side for the eight connections that arrive and the seven that leave. The diagonal cells hold the variable icons. Each filled cell shows its sign and a short stroke in its evidence texture. R10 draws as a staircase through C1, C2, C3 and C4: across from C1 to the C2 column, down to the diagonal, across to C3, and on around back to C1. Its one dashed step sits in the cell for L53, "Officials safe from defeat write rules for themselves". The R10 page lists the four cells as four sentences in reading order.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail is the core table in miniature with one staircase on it, and the blocks for the areas the loop touches widen so short loops stay readable. The corners sit at real cells, so the outline depends on which variables the loop visits and in what order. Loops inside one area make small steps near the diagonal. R1 and R5 make large steps between A and E, and R12 climbs from D to C and back. Two-step loops draw as rectangles, longer loops as stairs with more turns.

**Ground and red.** White ground, Stone grid, Charcoal type, Prussian and Verdigris staircases. The one red outlines a cell. In the sketch it is L25, "Decisive primaries reduce general-election pressure", where R-01 acts.

**Risks.** Tables put off many first-time readers, and a grid of dots looks like a spreadsheet at first sight. The staircase is exact but needs a sentence of teaching before it reads as a loop. The full 51 by 51 table cannot fit a phone, so phones get one block at a time. Empty cells dominate: 118 connections fill under 5 percent of the 2,601 cells.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## 4. Honeycomb

Fifty-one equal tiles in territories, the way newsrooms draw states on election night.

**Big idea.** Election-night hex maps taught a wide public that a tile can stand for a place, and that equal tiles keep big places from drowning small ones. Here each variable gets one hexagon with its icon. Areas become territories of touching tiles with firm borders, set apart by a channel of empty cells, and the placement puts connected variables side by side wherever it can. The overview draws no lines. It reads like a map of a country made of islands, and each tile opens its own page.

**Overview.** All 51 tiles show at once, grouped into territories that each carry a letter tab named in a key. Each tile carries its icon and a row of small beads, one per loop that passes through it, so policy tilt to insiders carries 7. Connections stay hidden until a tile is chosen; then its links draw as short arrows to neighbors and longer arcs to tiles farther off. The overview leaves out every line and every sign. The reader clicks a territory to open it or a tile to see its links.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking C enlarges its five tiles and greys the rest. Connections inside C draw as arrows between tile centers. R10 runs as a ring of arrows through safe seats, primary decisiveness, electoral pressure and incumbent control of election rules, and the step from electoral pressure to sanction applied draws in Verdigris for the balancing loop B4. Arrows from other territories enter at the border with their source tile named. The R10 page keeps the four tiles and draws each arrow in its evidence texture.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail crops the honeycomb around one loop, with the loop's tiles filled and a line through their centers. The silhouette of the filled tiles and the territories around it identify the loop. R8 fills two tiles of A, R10 four tiles of C, and R6 a bent run from A into B and E. Balancing loops fill in Verdigris. A reader who has seen the honeycomb once knows where each thumbnail points.

**Ground and red.** Newsprint ground, White tiles, Charcoal icons and borders, Prussian beads. The one red outlines a tile. In the sketch it is C1, safe seats, where R-03 (proportional elections) and R-46 (expand the House) act.

**Risks.** Hiding the lines hides what the atlas is about, so the first tap has to show connections at once. Equal tiles give a variable with two connections the same weight as policy tilt to insiders. Adjacency is a compromise, since 118 connections cannot all fall between neighbors, and readers may assume that touching tiles are connected. The honeycomb suits a phone because tiles are large, countable targets.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## 5. Kitchen Table

The household at the center, with every other variable placed by how many steps it takes to reach it.

**Big idea.** A first-time reader asks first how any of this touches their own life. The model gives an exact answer. Follow connections backward from households' financial security and every one of the 51 variables reaches it within six steps, and eight act on it directly. This direction puts that household at the center and draws a ring for each step out to six. Each variable sits on its ring inside a wedge for its area. Distance on the page then means one thing, and the reader can see that the courts sit three steps from the kitchen table and safe seats five.

**Overview.** Six rings, ten wedges and 51 points. Each point links to one variable on the next ring inward through the connection that carries it closer, so a reader can trace any variable home. Where several links qualify, the drawing picks one in a loop first and then the best evidence grade. All other connections stay hidden. Ring numbers read one to six. The center disc holds the household icon. Readers click a wedge to open an area or a ring to list everything at that distance.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking C keeps the rings and swings the C wedge to the right, wide enough for icons and labels. Its five variables sit on four rings: incumbent control of election rules on the third, primary decisiveness and electoral pressure on the fourth, safe seats on the fifth and sanction applied on the sixth. Lines show the route each one takes inward. R10 draws as a lasso across rings three to five. The R10 page keeps the faint rings behind the loop so the reader sees how far it sits from home, and it names the shortest route from the loop to the household.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail crops the rings to the stretch between the household and the loop, with the loop drawn at its true ring distances. The wedges the loop touches widen so short loops stay readable. Loops close to home sit beside the center dot, and R5 passes through it. Loops in government and elections float on the outer rings, and loops that cross areas stretch across wedges. Each loop gets its own radial profile.

**Ground and red.** White ground, Stone rings, Charcoal points, Prussian loops. The one red rings the center, households' financial security, the point the headline names. Five reforms act on it directly: R-23, R-24, R-25, R-26 and R-31.

**Risks.** Step counts say nothing about strength, so a variable two steps away can look more important than one five steps away with better evidence. Rings can look like layers, and readers may take them for levels of society. Centering one variable tilts the atlas toward household money; the same frame can center any variable on request. On a phone the six rings fit as a dial, and wedge labels move into a swipe list.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## 6. The Spine

All 51 variables on one line, with each connection as an arc; feedback shows as arcs that run back.

**Big idea.** Put the variables on one line, ordered from the outside drivers through technology and the economy layer to the core areas A to E. Then 74 connections run forward along the line and 44 run back. Forward arcs bow above the line and backward arcs below it. Any loop must contain at least one backward arc, so the reader finds every piece of feedback on one side of the line. On a phone the line runs down the screen, and the reader scrolls through the system from causes to consequences.

**Overview.** The line carries 51 ticks grouped by area, with area codes on a band beneath. Arcs in loops draw in Prussian and Verdigris, all others in thin Stone. The overview leaves out names and signs and keeps the arcs, which form a skyline above the line and a second one hanging below it. The reader clicks an area segment or a backward arc.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking C opens its five-tick segment wide, with icons and names beside each tick. Arcs inside C stay full size, and arcs to other areas shrink to stubs labeled with their far end. R10 shows as three forward arcs, C1 to C2, C2 to C3 and C3 to C4, and one backward arc, C4 to C1. The R10 page turns the segment into a column and keeps the backward step on its own side.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail draws the line short and plain with one loop's arcs, and widens the segments the loop touches. Which segment the arcs sit over, how wide they span and how many sit above or below give each loop its signature. Loops inside one area make small humps. R1, R5, R6 and R15 throw a long backward arc from legitimacy back to the economic structure.

**Ground and red.** Newsprint ground, Charcoal ticks, Prussian and Verdigris loop arcs, Stone for the rest. The one red marks an arc. In the sketch it is L03, "Policy advantages add to wealth at the top", the backward arc that closes R1 and the place where R-28 acts.

**Risks.** Arc diagrams are abstract, and the picture depends on the order chosen; another order gives another skyline. Long arcs dominate the view, though their length only measures distance in the list. With 118 arcs the core end of the line gets dense. The spine suits a phone better than any other direction, because it scrolls in one dimension.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## 7. Flywheels and Governors

Loops as machines: reinforcing loops are flywheels, balancing loops are governors.

**Big idea.** Engineers built two devices that behave like the two kinds of loop. A flywheel keeps turning the way it was pushed, as a reinforcing loop keeps a change going up or down. A governor, the pair of spinning weights on a steam engine, pushes back harder the faster the engine runs, as a balancing loop does. This direction makes loops the main objects. Each of the 23 loops is a wheel, and wheels that share a variable connect through a pin named for it. The machine shows how loops lock together: seven turn on the pin for policy tilt to insiders, and four on each of the pins for electoral pressure and incumbent control of election rules.

**Overview.** The overview shows 23 wheels and 16 pins, one pin for each variable that sits in two or more loops. Rods join each wheel to the pins it shares. Reinforcing wheels carry spokes and a heavy Prussian rim. Balancing wheels carry a small governor inside a Verdigris rim. Each rim splits into one arc per step, sized by delay and drawn in its evidence texture, so a loop with long delays and weak evidence has long, broken arcs. R8, B2 and B3 share no variable with any other loop and stand apart. Variables in no loop are left out. Readers click a wheel to open a loop or a pin to open a variable.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking area C gathers the seven wheels that pass through it: R10, R4a, R12, R4b, R11, R16 and B4, hung on the pins for safe seats, electoral pressure and incumbent control of election rules. The five C variables line up along the top with their icons. R10 is a four-spoke flywheel whose rim arcs run under two years, under two years, 2 to 10 years and 2 to 10 years, and its third arc is dashed. The R10 page turns the wheel slowly on load and sets each step's sentence beside its arc.

**Loop thumbnails.** The thumbnail is the wheel itself. Spoke count gives the number of steps, arc length gives the delays, texture gives the evidence and the inner drawing gives the type. A mark at the hub shows the loop's state from the model: a chevron up or down for loops running up or down, a brake bar for switched off, an open hub for the design target. R13 is a two-spoke flywheel with one short arc and one long one. B1a is a governor with a brake bar, because the model lists it as switched off.

**Ground and red.** Newsprint ground, Charcoal spokes and pins, Prussian flywheel rims, Verdigris governors. The one red marks a rim arc. In the sketch it is the step in B1a from oversight back to policy favoritism (L16), where R-08 and R-15 act, on the one governor the model lists as switched off.

**Risks.** Machines suggest fixed ratios and certain outcomes, and the model claims neither. Readers may take spin as speed or meshing as cause, so the drawing uses pins and rods and avoids gear teeth. The view leaves out the 26 variables in no loop and the 72 connections outside loops, so inputs from outside the loops wait one click down. On a phone the wheel thumbnails hold up well, and the full machine needs pinch zoom.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## 8. Crossroads

Loops as petals growing from the variables they share, printed like a cyanotype.

**Big idea.** Loops in this model cluster on a few variables. Policy tilt to insiders sits in 7 loops, and electoral pressure and incumbent control of election rules in four each. A loop that leaves a variable and returns to it draws as a petal. Gathering petals on their shared variable turns each hub into a rosette, and the system becomes a small garden of them. The style borrows from nineteenth-century cyanotype plates of plants, white on Prussian blue, a blue the brand already owns.

**Overview.** Each loop draws once, anchored at the station it shares with the most loops. That gives seven petals at policy tilt to insiders, four at incumbent control of election rules, three at electoral pressure and two at executive unilateralism, plus five single petals and two sketched seeds without a hub. Petal length follows the loop's total delay and its width the number of steps. The 26 variables in no loop stand as a column of small icons in the margin, and a tap on one draws its lines to the hubs it pushes. Readers click a hub to open its rosette or a petal to open a loop.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking area C opens the two rosettes rooted there. Incumbent control of election rules carries R4a, R4b, R10 and R12, and electoral pressure carries R11, R16 and B4. R10 grows from the first as a four-station petal running out through safe seats and primary decisiveness to electoral pressure and back. Electoral pressure also roots its own rosette, so a fine thread joins the two places it appears. The R10 page lifts the petal free and labels each station with icon and name.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail is one petal with its hub icon at the base, turned to the angle it takes in its rosette. Length, width, station count and edge textures come from the loop, and balancing petals fill with Verdigris. R13 is short and narrow, R6 long and wide, and R2 is dotted along both edges because both its steps are hypotheses. Petals from the same hub share a base icon, which groups them on the all-loops page.

**Ground and red.** Prussian ground with White and Newsprint line work. Red never sits on Prussian, so the red lives on a Newsprint label tag pinned to the hub the headline names. In the sketch the tag marks policy tilt to insiders, where R-09 and R-27 act.

**Risks.** Anchoring each loop at one hub means a variable such as electoral pressure appears in more than one place, which can confuse a first-time reader. Petal length may read as importance. A Prussian page with white line work is striking and dark, so long reading moves to Newsprint pages one click down. On a phone one rosette fills the screen and the garden becomes a swipe between hubs.

## 9. Open Atlas

The system as a two-page spread: the economy on the left, democracy on the right, technology in the fold.

**Big idea.** The atlas models democracy and the economy as one causal system, and a book spread can show exactly that. The left page holds economic structure and the economy layer, 18 variables. The right page holds political inputs, electoral competition, governing institutions and legitimacy, 20 variables. Technology touches both and sits in the fold, and the outside drivers sit in the top margin. Twelve connections cross the fold, five toward democracy and seven toward the economy, and four of those seven start at policy tilt to insiders. The crossings carry the atlas's argument, and the spread puts them in the middle of the page.

**Overview.** Each page shows its variables as icons in small groups by area. Connections within a page draw thin and Stone, and the twelve crossings draw heavier in Charcoal. Four loops cross the fold, R1, R5, R6 and R15, and they draw in Prussian. R8 stays on the economy page and every other loop stays on the democracy page. Technology's eight variables stack in the fold with lines out to both sides. Readers click a page group to open an area, or a crossing to read it.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking area C turns to a right-hand page given over to elections. Its five variables spread across the page, and the eight incoming and seven outgoing connections run to the page edges labeled with the page and area at their far end. R10 sits wholly on this page. The R10 page shows the loop as a closed circuit in the middle of the right-hand page, with a note that none of its steps crosses to the economy.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail crops the spread around one loop, showing page edges and the fold wherever the loop reaches them, with the loop drawn at its stations' positions. Loops that cross the fold read at once: R1, R5, R6 and R15 cross twice each. R8 sits alone on the left page. The rest sit on the right in different places, with R10 and R4a high in the elections group and R13 and R18 lower down in government.

**Ground and red.** Newsprint ground with two White pages, Charcoal crossings, Prussian loops and Stone for the rest. The one red marks a crossing. In the sketch it is L31, "Insider-tilted labor rules weaken worker bargaining", the step that closes both R6 and R15 and the place where R-21 acts.

**Risks.** Putting economic structure on the economy page is a choice. Area A could sit on either side, and the count of crossings depends on it. Pages suggest two separate books, so the fold has to look busy. Technology in the fold may read as the hinge of the whole system, which the model does not claim. On a phone the spread becomes two pages with the fold between them as a strip of crossings.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## 10. Tabletop Model

An architect's model of the system, each variable a block whose height counts the loops through it.

**Big idea.** People read a physical model faster than a diagram. They see what stands tall and look around it. This direction builds the system as a tabletop model in isometric view. Each area is a low plinth, and each variable is a block on it. Block height has one meaning, the number of loops that pass through the variable, so policy tilt to insiders rises 7 units and the 26 variables in no loop lie flat. Loop steps arc between block tops. The reader sees where feedback concentrates from the skyline alone.

**Overview.** Eight plinths sit on the table: the five core areas, the outside drivers, the economy layer and technology. The overview shows all 51 blocks with icons on their tops and the loop arcs between them. Connections outside loops stay out until a block is chosen. Each plinth carries a letter at its corner, keyed beside the table. Readers click a plinth to lift it from the table or a block to open the variable.

**Close-ups: area C and R10.** Clicking C lifts its plinth forward and turns it to face the reader. Its five blocks stand three, one, four, four and one units tall: safe seats, primary decisiveness, electoral pressure, incumbent control of election rules and sanction applied. R10 arcs across the first four. Arcs to other plinths stay as stubs. The R10 page sets the four blocks alone on a small plinth with the loop's arcs over them and the step sentences on cards in front.

**Loop thumbnails.** Each thumbnail is a small plinth holding only the loop's blocks, at their real heights and their relative places on the table. R10 shows two four-unit towers beside a three-unit block and a one-unit block. R5 shows a seven-unit tower beside a three-unit block and a one-unit block. R8 shows two short blocks side by side. The skyline of each loop is its signature.

**Ground and red.** White ground, Newsprint and Stone plinths, White blocks with Charcoal edges, Prussian and Verdigris arcs. The one red marks the top of a block. In the sketch it is electoral pressure, which sits in four loops and where R-02, R-07 and R-47 act.

**Risks.** Height invites readers to read the block as the amount of the variable, such as how much electoral pressure exists, which the model does not measure. Isometric views hide short blocks behind tall ones, so the camera has to turn. A 3D view costs more to build and to make accessible. On a phone the table fits as a picture, but blocks become small targets, so the first tap picks a plinth.

**Sketch.** Drawn from the model data at 640 by 400.

## Ranking

I ranked the ten on what a first-time reader needs: a whole picture they can take in, loops they can tell apart, honesty about what the model claims, and a version that works on a phone.

1. **Great Circle.** It shows the circle the core areas form in one shape, and every loop thumbnail is that loop drawn on the same ring.
2. **Star Atlas.** The most beautiful of the ten, and the loop page becomes a page of distinct, named figures.
3. **Open Atlas.** It puts the publication's own argument, that democracy and the economy form one system, in the middle of the page.
4. **Kitchen Table.** The best first door for a member of the public: it starts at the household and measures everything from there.
5. **Flywheels and Governors.** It teaches the difference between feeding itself and pushing back through objects people already know.
6. **Honeycomb.** The most phone-ready overview, in a form the public already knows from election coverage.
7. **Crossroads.** Striking and true to where loops meet, but showing one variable in two places will trip up new readers.
8. **The Spine.** Exact and easy to scroll, held back by abstraction and by its dependence on one chosen order.
9. **Ledger Grid.** The most exact drawing of the model, best kept as an expert view because its first screen asks a lot of a new reader.
10. **Tabletop Model.** An appealing skyline, but height will be read as amount, and it costs the most to build.

## The six I would build

- **Great Circle.** It shows the circle the core areas form in one shape, and every loop thumbnail is that loop drawn on the same ring.
- **Star Atlas.** The most beautiful of the ten, and the loop page becomes a page of distinct, named figures.
- **Open Atlas.** It puts the publication's own argument, that democracy and the economy form one system, in the middle of the page.
- **Kitchen Table.** The best first door for a member of the public: it starts at the household and measures everything from there.
- **Flywheels and Governors.** It teaches the difference between feeding itself and pushing back through objects people already know.
- **Honeycomb.** The most phone-ready overview, in a form the public already knows from election coverage.

Together the six give the client six different structures to choose among: a ring, a sky, a book spread, a dial centered on the household, a machine of loops and a tiled map.

## Notes on method

- Every label, sign, grade, delay and count in the sketches and in this text comes from the model file. The script computes the headline counts from the model at build time and asserts the reach figure. I checked the hand-written counts (loop lengths, shared loop pairs, reform targets) against the model with separate queries.
- Star Atlas, Flywheels and Governors and Honeycomb place things with seeded layout routines. Positions in those sketches carry no meaning beyond what each pitch states.
- The loop record for R10 lists three reforms (R-01, R-02, R-03). Two more, R-46 and R-47, name R10 among their loops, so a close-up should show all five.
- Tested: the script runs, every SVG text is 13 px or larger, and the page renders at 1440 px wide. Not tested: phone layouts, motion and interaction, which these sketches only describe.
